# DFXM Pipeline — Codebase Reference

> [!info] What this is
> A file-by-file, part-by-part explanation of the **entire** codebase: what each
> module, class, and function does and how the pieces fit together. For *how to
> use* the app see [[Usage]]; for contributor conventions see `CLAUDE.md`. This
> document focuses on *what the code is*.

> [!note] Keep this in sync
> When you add/remove a module, class, or public function — or change what one
> does — update the matching entry here in the same change (see
> [[#Maintaining this reference]]).

## Big picture

The codebase is three layers with a strict one-way dependency:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph GUI["gui/  (PySide6, Qt)"]
        APP[app / main_window] --> SV[stage_view]
        SV --> WID[widgets/*]
        SV --> BIND[bindings] --> REG
    end
    subgraph CORE["dfxm/  (Qt-free core)"]
        REG[stages/registry] --> ST[stages/*]
        ST --> COM[common/*]
        ST --> CFG[config/*]
        RUN[runner] --> REG
    end
    GUI -->|runs stages via| RUN
    TESTS["tests/*"] --> CORE
    TESTS --> GUI
```

> [!important] The core invariant
> **`dfxm/` never imports Qt.** The GUI imports the core; the core never imports
> the GUI. That is what lets every stage run headless (CLI/tests) and keeps the
> stage **worker process** lightweight. Heavy optional deps (`pyvista`/`vtk`)
> are imported *lazily*, inside the functions that need them.

Three ideas recur everywhere:

1. **Schema-driven stages.** A stage is a pure function `run(params, progress=None)
   -> result` plus a declarative `STAGE: StageSpec`. The GUI builds its form from
   the schema; the CLI and tests call `run` directly.
2. **One shared alignment.** Every volume stage co-registers through the *single*
   implementation in [[#`alignment.py`]] (origin-0 PVTI world frame). No stage
   re-implements the samy-shift / Z-interpolation. The in-core `align_volume`
   and the block-wise `align_volume_streamed` are the same code: the former is a
   façade that drains the latter.
3. **Process isolation.** Long/`matplotlib`/3-D work runs in a child process via
   [[#`dfxm/runner.py` — the process worker]] so the UI stays responsive and
   cancellable.

---

## Repository layout

```
dfxm_pipeline/
├── dfxm/                  # Layer 1 — Qt-free core library
│   ├── config/            #   typed config models + YAML presets
│   ├── common/            #   shared primitives (sort, h5io, alignment, plotting, figures, …)
│   ├── stages/            #   the 9 analysis stages + registry
│   ├── runner.py          #   run a stage in a child process
│   └── viewer_jobs.py     #   child-process viewer jobs (e.g. rotation video)
├── gui/                   # Layer 2 — PySide6 desktop app
│   └── widgets/           #   reusable Qt widgets (incl. export_dialog)
├── tests/                 # Layer 3 — pytest suite + fixtures
├── experiments/           # shipped experiment presets (YAML)
├── docs/                  # Usage.md (user) + Codebase.md (this file)
├── CLAUDE.md  README.md  pyproject.toml  .claude/settings.json  .gitignore
```

---

## Layer 1 — `dfxm/` core library

`dfxm/__init__.py` — package marker; defines `__version__` and states the
**Qt-free invariant** in its docstring.

### `dfxm/config` — typed config & presets

#### `models.py`

The declarative backbone. Nothing here touches Qt; the GUI *reads* these types
to build forms.

| Symbol | Kind | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `ParamType` | `str, Enum` | The editor kinds: `INT, FLOAT, STR, BOOL, PATH, DIR, SAVE_PATH, ENUM, TEXT`. The GUI maps each to a widget. `TEXT` = multi-line (JSON). |
| `Param` | frozen dataclass | One parameter: `name, type, label, default, unit, choices, help, calibration`. GUI metadata: `advanced` (True → collapse into the Advanced expander), `group` (themed header inside the Advanced section), `must_exist` (True → the GUI verifies the path exists on disk before a run). ROI-picker hooks: `roi_group` (non-empty string → this param belongs to a named ROI-picker target; all params sharing a `roi_group` are edited together), `roi_axis` (`"" | "x" | "y" | "both"` — which spatial axis the param constrains; `"both"` means one 4-int `r0,r1,c0,c1` field), `roi_frame` (`"" | "detector" | "map"` — the coordinate frame a ROI param is expressed in; `"detector"` = raw-detector pixels, `"map"` = darfix-map pixels relative to the darfix window. A ROI-carrying param declares `roi_group` and/or `roi_frame`; downstream code that must locate every markable ROI field keys off `p.roi_group or p.roi_frame`). `__post_init__` enforces: (1) `ENUM` has `choices`; (2) non-empty `roi_axis` requires non-empty `roi_group`; (3) `roi_axis` ∈ `{"", "x", "y", "both"}`; (4) `roi_frame` ∈ `{"", "detector", "map"}`. `coerce(value)` converts a raw form string to the declared type. |
| `StageSpec` | frozen dataclass | A stage's identity + its `params` tuple. `defaults()` → dict of defaults; `get(name)` → a `Param`; `coerce_all(values)` → all values coerced with defaults filled in. `estimate: str \| None` — an optional `"module:function"` target naming a cost-estimator function, kept as a **string** (not a callable) so building the spec never imports the estimator's module. `estimator()` resolves it lazily via `dfxm.stages.registry.resolve` and returns `None` when `estimate` is unset. |
| `CostEstimate` | frozen dataclass | What a stage run will cost, computed from HDF5 shapes alone (`.shape`/`.dtype`, never data — cheap enough to recompute on every form change): `peak_bytes` (in-core high-water mark, including transient copies — a `[:].astype(np.float64)` on a float32 source costs 3x the on-disk size), `input_bytes`, `shape: tuple[int, ...] \| None`, `chunkable` (False for irreducibly whole-array work that must run disk-backed instead), `note: str \| None`. Returned by the callable a `StageSpec.estimator()` resolves. |
| `Experiment` | dataclass | The shared, preset-saved state: data roots, folder glob patterns (`folder_pattern`, `mosa_pattern`, `rocking_pattern`), calibration (`ccmth_ref_deg`, pixel scales), regions of interest (`darfix_roi` — the darfix detector crop as `x,y,w,h` origin+size; `analysis_roi_x` / `analysis_roi_y` — the map-frame analysis window as `c0,c1` / `r0,r1`, blank = full; frames + conversions in `dfxm/common/roi.py`), and beamline HDF5/motor paths. `to_dict()` / `from_dict()` (the latter warns on unknown keys). |
| `EXPERIMENT_SCHEMA` | `tuple[Param]` | The display schema for `Experiment`, in field order. A test asserts it stays in lock-step with the dataclass fields. |
| `CALIBRATION_FIELDS` | tuple[str] | Names of the physically-meaningful fields (flagged red in the form). |
| `experiment_schema()` | fn | Returns `EXPERIMENT_SCHEMA`. |

`tests/test_param_metadata.py` enforces the metadata contract: every `Param` must have a `help` string, every advanced param must have a `group`, and every stage declares between 1 and 8 essential (non-advanced) params.

#### `presets.py`

Load/save/discover experiment presets (YAML in `experiments/`).

| Function | What it does |
|---|---|
| `experiments_dir()` | Default presets dir = `<project root>/experiments`. |
| `discover_experiments(dir=None)` | `{name → path}` for every `*.yaml` (name from the YAML `name:` key, else the file stem); sorted. |
| `load_experiment(path)` | Read one YAML → `Experiment`. |
| `save_experiment(exp, path)` | Write an `Experiment` to YAML (field order preserved; comments not emitted). |
| `load_experiment_by_name(name, dir=None)` | Discover + load by name. |

**`detect.py` — data-driven experiment initialization.** Qt-free detectors that
suggest experiment values from the trees on disk; each returns `Detection`
rows (`field`, `value`, `note`, `error` — `error` set = skip-with-reason;
`value` and `error` both `None` = info-only row). `folder_families` /
`detect_patterns` classify `<stem>__<N>` raw subfolders into
folder/mosa/rocking globs; `select_scan_file` picks the raw scan .h5 (concat
output excluded); `detect_entry_suffix` reads the majority BLISS suffix;
`detect_pixel_sizes` wraps `common.pixel_size.compute_pixel_size`;
`find_strain_maps` + `detect_ccmth_from_maps` take the nanmedian of the ccmth
COM map (mosa maps are skipped — chi/mu only), with
`detect_ccmth_from_positioners` as the pre-darfix fallback;
`detect_darfix_roi` recovers the crop *size* from the map shape (darfix
records no origin — blank current → partial `?,?,w,h`, filled current →
consistency check). `detect_experiment(current)` orchestrates all of the
above (re-runnable, never overwrites); `main` is the
`python3 -m dfxm.config.detect` CLI. GUI consumer:
`gui/widgets/detect_review.py` + the Edit dialog's "Initialize from data…"
button.

### `dfxm/common` — shared primitives

`common/__init__.py` is just a package marker. The rest are the de-duplicated
building blocks the legacy scripts each re-implemented. New in the plot-export
workstream: `plotting.py` gained the `PlotStyle` dataclass and publication
primitives; `figures.py` was added as the stage-figure catalog.

#### `sort.py`
- `natural_sort_key(s)` — orders embedded numbers numerically (`layer__2` before `layer__10`).
- `find_matching_folders(root, pattern)` — directories matching a glob, natural-sorted by basename.
- `resolve_layer_work(params, *, maps_filename) -> list[str]` — the `<folder>/<maps_filename>` paths a folder-based stage (strain, mosaicity) would process, mirroring `run()`'s own resolution (`mode="single"` → `input_folder`; `"batch"` → `root_folder` + `folder_pattern` via `find_matching_folders`). Returns `[]` instead of raising when nothing resolves; used by those stages' `estimate()` functions.

#### `errors.py`
- `StageUserError(message, hint="")` — a `ValueError` subclass that marks an input problem the user can fix (wrong path, missing file, bad parameter). Stages raise it at their input-validation choke points. The runner captures it and forwards `hint` to the GUI, which displays both `message` and `hint` in the status banner so the user knows what to change. `tests/test_stage_user_errors.py` checks that each stage raises this (not a bare exception) for malformed inputs.

#### `h5io.py`
Stage-agnostic HDF5 I/O (used mostly by [[#concat.py]]).

| Function / type | What it does |
|---|---|
| `resolve_input_file(folder, override)` | BLISS convention: `folder/folder.h5` (or an override name). |
| `make_output_path(in, suffix="_concat")` | `<stem><suffix><ext>` next to the input. |
| `get_filtered_entries(h5f, suffix)` | Top-level groups ending in `suffix` (e.g. `.1`), natural-sorted. |
| `make_virtual_source(ds, out, policy)` | A `VirtualSource` referencing `ds` by **relative** (portable) or **absolute** path. |
| `build_virtual_layout(sources, counts, shape, dtype)` | Stack per-scan detector sources along axis 0 into one `VirtualLayout`. |
| `detector_info(ds)` | `(n_frames, frame_shape, dtype)` for a 3-D stack. |
| `read_positioners(h5f, group)` | Every dataset under a positioners group → `{name: value}` (raw). |
| `read_samy_samz(h5f, group, …)` | The `samy`/`samz` stages specifically. |
| `MapsValidation` + `validate_maps_file(path, required)` | Bracket darfix: check a `maps.h5` has the required COM datasets *without raising* (returns a result object that is truthy when valid). |
| `sum_dataset_bytes(path) -> (total_bytes, largest_shape, largest_itemsize)` | Total in-memory size of every dataset in *path*, from shapes alone (`f.visititems`, reads no data). `(0, None, 0)` for anything unreadable. Used by the `slices`/`paraview`/`visualize` `estimate()` functions for their file-level `input_bytes` figure. |
| `iter_dataset_sizes(path) -> list[(name, shape, itemsize)]` | Same shapes-only `f.visititems` walk as `sum_dataset_bytes`, but returns one `(in-file path, shape, itemsize)` entry per dataset instead of a running total — lets a caller size the *specific* dataset it is about to load rather than the whole file. `[]` for anything unreadable. Used by `slices.estimate()` to size each individually-selected (`include_*`-toggled) volume. |
| `StackedVolumeFile(path, *, compression="gzip")` | Builds a `(Z, Y, X)` volume file **one layer at a time**, so a stage that stacks per-layer maps peaks at one layer instead of the whole volume (plus `np.stack`'s copy). See below. |

**`StackedVolumeFile`** — the shared incremental writer behind
[[#strain.py]]`.run` and [[#mosaicity.py]]`.run`, which previously collected every
layer in a list and `np.stack`'d it at the end.

- `append(dataset_path, layer)` — add one 2-D `layer` to `dataset_path`, creating the dataset on first use as `shape=(0, *layer.shape)`, `maxshape=(None, *layer.shape)` (the layer axis is **unlimited**, which is what lets it grow), `chunks=(1, *layer.shape)` — one chunk per layer, matching how every downstream reader accesses these volumes — with the writer's compression (`compression_opts=4` for gzip), then `resize`-and-write one more row. `dataset_path` may be a nested path (`"chi/Center of mass"`); intermediate groups are created implicitly by h5py, so a group only exists when it actually holds a dataset. Raises `ValueError` when a layer's **shape** disagrees with the rows already written (`"<path>: maps have differing shapes {…}; fix ROI"`) or when its **dtype** does — the dataset's dtype is fixed by the first layer, so a wider later layer would be silently truncated on write where `np.stack` promoted. Per-dataset layer counts may legitimately differ (a mosaicity layer can be missing one of the four maps).
- `shape(dataset_path) -> tuple[int, ...]` — the dataset's current `(Z, Y, X)`; `datasets() -> list[str]` — the appended paths, sorted; `set_attrs(**attrs)` — file-level attributes. `shape` and `set_attrs` raise `ValueError` before the first `append` (there is no file yet) rather than dropping the attributes silently.
- **Opens lazily**, on the first `append` rather than in `__init__`, so a stage can construct the writer *above* its layer loop — which is what keeps the loop's peak at one layer — without touching the filesystem for a run that produces no layers. That matters for single mode pointed at a folder that does not exist (a stale, session-persisted `Input folder`): the run must come back as a `result.skipped` entry, not a raw `FileNotFoundError` from creating a part file in a missing directory. **Construction** (not opening) unlinks any `<path>.part` left from an earlier run — the runner SIGTERM/SIGKILLs the child on Cancel, so a cancelled run orphans its part file, and this pipeline runs one stage at a time (one child per run, GUI-serialised), so a part file already sitting there is by definition dead. Doing it at construction rather than at open is what makes reclamation unconditional: a re-run that goes on to produce no layers never opens anything, so an open-time unlink would leave the orphan (gigabytes, in the experiment root) exactly where it was. Deleting the orphan and then failing leaves the user with neither file — deliberate, since a truncated `.part` is unusable either way. Construction still creates nothing: no file, and no directory for a `path` whose parent is missing (a `FileNotFoundError` from the unlink is swallowed along with the ordinary "no orphan" case).
- `close()` — flush, close, and `os.replace` the working `<path>.part` onto `<path>`. `abort()` — close and unlink the part file, swallowing errors so it never masks the caller's exception. Both are **no-ops once the file has been committed or aborted** (`_closed`), and both are no-ops when nothing was ever appended. That is what makes the stages' "no layers produced" path legal: they call `abort()` and `return` from inside the `with`, leaving `__exit__` to call `close()` on a part file that is already gone. A *first* `close()` still propagates whatever failed — the flag suppresses repeat calls, never errors.
- Context manager: commit on a clean exit, abort on an exception — so a failure mid-run leaves neither the output nor a stray `.part`, the same all-or-nothing behaviour the write-at-the-end version had for free. Writing to a part file also means a failed re-run no longer truncates the *previous* volume, which the old `h5py.File(path, "w")` did before it knew the write would succeed.

#### `pixel_size.py` (new)
`compute_pixel_size(scan_h5, positioners_path="instrument/positioners",
entry_suffix=".1") -> PixelSizeResult`. Reads the far-field geometry motors
(`mainx`, `obx`, `ffsel`, `ffz`, `lenssel`) from the first matching entry of a
raw (pre-darfix) scan and derives the effective detector pixel size. `mainx`
reads negative in the ID03 motor frame, so the formulas use its magnitude:
`M = |mainx|/obx − 1`, `E_x = base/M` (base 3.25 for 2× at `ffsel=−60`, 0.65 for
10× at `ffsel=0`), `2θ = atan2(ffz, |mainx|)`, and `E_y = E_x/sin(2θ)` when the
condenser is in (`lenssel=0`) else `E_y = E_x`. Raises `StageUserError` for a
missing entry/motor, an unrecognized `ffsel`, a non-positive `obx`, a
non-physical magnification, or (condenser in) a non-positive `2θ` (`ffz ≤ 0`) —
so a sign-flipped motor errors out instead of writing a negative pixel size.
`PixelSizeResult` carries both pixel sizes plus `magnification`,
`two_theta_deg`, `objective`, `condenser_in`, and the raw (signed) motor values.

#### `roi.py` (new)
Darfix-window / map-frame ROI conversions and validation. Pure module — no Qt,
no I/O. Two frames describe every DFXM dataset: the **darfix window** (the
detector crop darfix used when fitting the maps, given as *origin + size*
`x,y,w,h` — map pixel (0, 0) sits at detector pixel `(x, y)`) and the
**analysis window** (the sub-region chosen for study, in *map-frame* start,end
pairs). The rule tying them together is `detector = darfix_origin + map`.

| Function / type | What it does |
|---|---|
| `DarfixWindow(origin_x, origin_y, width, height)` | Frozen dataclass for the darfix crop; properties `.x0`/`.x1`/`.y0`/`.y1` give the absolute detector bounds (`x1 = origin_x + width`, etc.). |
| `parse_pair(text)` | `"start,end"` → `(start, end)`; blank/`None` → `None`; malformed → `ValueError`. |
| `parse_darfix_roi(text)` | `"x,y,w,h"` (origin+size, darfix's own display) → `DarfixWindow`; blank → `None`; malformed → `ValueError`. |
| `map_to_detector(pair, origin)` / `detector_to_map(pair, origin)` | Convert a `(start, end)` pair along one axis between map-frame and absolute detector pixels; inverses of each other. |
| `format_pair(pair)` | `(start, end)` → `"start,end"`. |
| `analysis_detector_window(darfix_roi, analysis_roi_x, analysis_roi_y)` | The analysis window in absolute detector pixels (what rocking crops) → `(det_x, det_y)`, each `(start, end) \| None`. A blank analysis axis falls back to the full darfix window; no darfix window at all → `(None, None)`. Malformed input raises `ValueError` — use `validate_rois` for user-facing messages instead. |
| `validate_rois(darfix_roi, analysis_roi_x, analysis_roi_y)` | Human-readable problems with the three experiment ROI fields (`[]` = all fine): malformed text, a non-positive darfix width/height, `end <= start` or `start < 0` on an analysis pair, or an analysis end past the darfix window's own size. |

#### `eta.py` (new)
Qt-free ETA estimation for progress readouts. Pure module — no Qt, no I/O.

| Symbol | What it does |
|---|---|
| `format_eta(elapsed_s, frac)` | One-shot human remaining-time estimate from a single `(elapsed_s, frac)` sample: `""` when `frac < 0.05`, `elapsed_s < 2.0`, or `frac >= 1.0` (too early/noisy/finished to say anything); else `"~{s} s left"` under 90 s remaining, else `"~{m} min left"`. |
| `EtaEstimator(clock=time.monotonic)` | Smoothed estimator for a running progress reporter. `reset()` starts a new `t0` and forgets prior samples; `update(frac)` clamps `frac` to `[0, 1]`, ignores a regressing fraction (never poisons the estimate), and EMA-smooths the raw remaining-time extrapolation (`0.7` weight on the previous smoothed value); `eta_text()` returns `""` until the same 5 %-done / 2 s-elapsed thresholds as `format_eta` are met, else the formatted remaining time. |

Consumed by the GUI's busy overlay/progress text (`gui/widgets/busy.py`, `gui/stage_view.py`) but importable and testable without Qt.

#### `_glprobe.py` (new)
Out-of-process OpenGL capability probe, run as `python -m dfxm.common._glprobe`.
Prints one JSON object (`status`, `renderer`, `vendor`, `version`,
`max_3d_texture`, `error`) and always exits 0. Kept a leaf module (pyvista
imported inside `probe()`) because a broken GL driver segfaults rather than
raising, and because `spawn` re-imports the child's module. Reports the raw
renderer string only — classifying it as software vs. hardware belongs to
`machine.is_software_renderer`.

#### `machine.py` (new)
`MachineProfile` / `GLInfo` plus the probes behind them (`probe_cpu`,
`probe_memory`, `probe_disk`, `probe_ffmpeg`, `is_software_renderer`,
`profile`). Every probe is individually wrapped: `profile()` never raises, and
unmeasured fields report `None`/`0` with the reason in `probe_errors`. `psutil`
is used when importable, with `os.sysconf` / `GlobalMemoryStatusEx` fallbacks.
GL is probed out of process (`_glprobe.py`) via `probe_gl()`, memoised and
cached to `~/.cache/dfxm/gl_probe.json` (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\dfxm\cache` on
Windows) keyed on OS/host/python/vtk. A child that segfaults, hangs or emits
garbage yields `gl_status="crashed"` rather than an exception. `profile()`
skips GL unless `probe_gl_now=True`.

#### `advice.py` (new)
Machine-aware policy: pure functions over a `MachineProfile` and a
`CostEstimate` deciding what a run should actually do, with no Qt and no IO of
its own. `RunPlan` / `Advice` are the frozen result dataclasses. `headroom_bytes`
takes the tighter of two limits — 60% of available RAM (guards against other
processes) and 50% of total RAM (guards against a misleading "available" behind
a large page cache). `working_set_budget_bytes(profile, *, rss_floor_bytes)` is
**the number to pass as `alignment.align_volume_streamed`'s `budget_bytes`**,
never `headroom_bytes` itself: headroom is RSS and `budget_bytes` is
`tracemalloc` (Python allocations, carrying neither the interpreter, the
extension modules, h5py's chunk cache, a memmap's resident pages nor allocator
fragmentation).

**The two are related additively, not by a ratio.** Measured for `paraview` over
18 runs (two shapes × budgets 16/64/512 MB × 1/8/16 Z pieces): `RSS = 230 MB +
1.18 × traced` (least squares, r² = 0.98), while the *ratio* RSS/traced over the
same runs ranged from **2.06 to 23.43** — no single multiplier can stand in for
it. The intercept is the process image `tracemalloc` never sees, and it dominates
every small run, which is why the ratio explodes as the traced peak shrinks; the
slope is allocations made inside extension libraries (chiefly VTK's deep copy of
each piece), resident but untraced. So the function solves `rss_floor_bytes +
MARGINAL_RSS_PER_TRACED_BYTE × traced ≤ headroom` for `traced`.
`MARGINAL_RSS_PER_TRACED_BYTE` (1.3) and the floors callers pass sit **above**
the least-squares line, so the model normally over-states RSS — but it is **not
an envelope and does not claim to be one**: at a *fixed* traced peak
(276.6–276.9 MB) measured RSS ranged over 45 MB (566–613 MB), so there is
untraced, blocking-dependent variance wider than any margin worth quoting, and a
large enough sweep will find a case the model under-states. Both terms are
therefore set with deliberate slack rather than fitted tight (over-stating costs
a slightly smaller budget; under-stating invites an OOM), and the slack is put in
the **floor**, because that is the term a stage can measure and pin. One further
thing is *not* claimed: the piece pass is bounded by `num_pieces_z` and not by
this budget at all (see `paraview.advisory_n_pieces`), so a run can exceed
headroom with the budget perfectly honoured.

`rss_floor_bytes` is **required and per stage** — it is set by what a stage
imports and differs by hundreds of MB between a VTK-importing stage and one that
only needs matplotlib, which is the same verdict the project's estimator gate
reached about its own per-stage error (visualize 1.67×, slices 1.66×, paraview
1.10× — *not* one universal factor). Each stage measures its own with
`tests/peak_rss.py` on trivial input, names it (e.g. `paraview.RSS_FLOOR_BYTES`)
and **pins it with `assert_floor_covers`** — the value is not implied by the
formula, so without that test `RSS_FLOOR_BYTES = 1` passes every other check
while making the budget far too large. `MARGINAL_RSS_PER_TRACED_BYTE` is by
contrast shared across stages, on the argument that it is a property of how numpy
and VTK allocate rather than of what is imported, and it errs safe (paraview's
per-piece VTK deep copy is probably the pipeline's heaviest slope, so a stage
measuring its own should come in below it). **Escalation trigger, recorded in the
code: any stage that measures a marginal above 1.3 makes this constant per-stage
too.** Shared until contradicted, not shared on principle — and the first
independent test did not contradict it: `visualize` (2026-08-21) fitted
`RSS = 108.7 MB + 1.249 x traced` (r² = 0.995) over twelve runs at three shapes
and four budgets, below 1.3 and with the fitted intercept landing on that
stage's independently measured bare process image (107.6 MB). The trap recorded
beside it: a marginal is only measurable over a **wide traced range** — a first
sweep that varied only the shape, with the 3-D render on, moved traced by 4% and
produced a meaningless slope of 1.53 at r² = 0.64 that would have tripped the
trigger for nothing. Returns at least
`MIN_STREAM_BUDGET_BYTES` (4 MB),
including when the floor alone exceeds the headroom: the alignment floors its
blocking at one output layer anyway, so a smaller number buys no memory and only
produces warnings. Deliberately not floored at `MIN_BUDGET_BYTES` — that floor
means "below this, do not bother chunking", and re-applying it here would hand
back an unconverted number on exactly the small machine the conversion protects.
**Unrelated to the ~1.66× by which `estimate().peak_bytes` under-predicted real
RSS**: that is estimator model error (a wrong guess at how much a stage
allocates), this is a currency conversion, and the two only look alike for being
dimensionless. Same *shape* of correction as
`volumeio.display_headroom_bytes`/`DISPLAY_COPIES`, different reason. `plan_run`
escalates in-core -> chunked -> disk-backed as
the estimate outgrows the headroom; nothing ever refuses to run for lack of
RAM, the one genuine blocker is running out of scratch disk, reported before
work starts. `advise_3d` downsamples a volume to fit
`GL_MAX_3D_TEXTURE_SIZE` and steers software-GL machines toward geometry
render modes (surface/isosurface upload geometry instead of one large 3-D
texture, so volume mode silently renders blank past the limit). Every decision
carries a plain-language reason string — written once here and reused by the
log, the GUI banner and the stage result notes.

#### `volumeio.py` (new)
Bounded-memory HDF5 volume reading. Pure module — no Qt, no stage-specific
logic. The governing guarantee for the **streaming** helpers (`iter_blocks`,
`iter_with_context`, `BlockReader`, `load_or_stream`, `dataset_blocks`,
`neumaier_sum`, `block_reduce`, `block_nansum`, `stream_mean`, `stream_minmax`,
`stream_quantile`, `two_pass`) is **budget-independence**: for any
`budget_bytes` they produce bit-identical results, so a laptop and a
workstation streaming under different budgets emit the same data product.
(`iter_with_context` earns its place by extension, and conditionally: its
*windows* are naturally budget-shaped, but the
interiors tile the source exactly once and each carries the successor rows a
forward-looking dependency needs, so the consumer's per-row answer is the same at
any budget — **provided the consumer conforms**, i.e. each output row depends on
at most `trailing` successor rows and on no cross-row reduction. That is a
constraint on the caller, not a property of the helper: a consumer that summed
down the window, or reached further ahead than it declared, would be
budget-dependent no matter what this yields. `iter_with_context` also carries the
one budget-dependent *failure* in the module: with `trailing > 1` the final short
block may be unable to supply the requested context, and whether it can depends
on the block width, so a workstation budget can succeed where a laptop's raises.
Unreachable at the default `trailing=1`.) One further qualification, on **how**
the guarantee is held rather than whether: under `axis=0` blocking a value's
position in the flattened stream does not move with the block width, so
`neumaier_sum`'s lane assignment is invariant *by construction*; under `axis=1`
— column blocking, which only `matched` uses — the ravel position does move, so
invariance there is **empirical**, unbroken across ~120 sweeps but held by
evidence rather than construction. A cross-budget check is required, not
optional, when adding an `axis=1` consumer. The three **display** helpers below
(`display_headroom_bytes`, `display_decimation`, `decimation_note`) are
explicitly *not* covered — they exist to coarsen a volume that cannot be
streamed, so a different budget yields a different stride and therefore
different data on screen. They never touch the stored file.

| Function / type | What it does |
|---|---|
| `volume_bytes(dset)` | In-memory size of an HDF5 dataset if fully loaded, in bytes. |
| `display_headroom_bytes()` | RAM a *display* (render) load may use here — `advice.headroom_bytes(machine.profile())`, imported lazily. |
| `display_decimation(dset, budget_bytes)` | Smallest power-of-two stride (≤ `MAX_DISPLAY_DECIMATION` = 16) bringing `dset` within `budget_bytes` for a render path, sized as float64 (`volume_bytes // itemsize * 8`, since render paths upcast whatever the stored dtype is). The budget is divided by `DISPLAY_COPIES` (**2**) because a render loader peaks at roughly two copies of the strided array, not one — the `.astype(float)` transient plus a second full-size array alongside `vol` (`vol[np.isfinite(vol)]` for the viewer's percentile clim, `Scene3D.prepared()`'s masked copy for the video). Budgeting one copy would let a volume just under headroom take stride 1 and then allocate ~2× headroom, i.e. the OOM the decimation exists to prevent. Requires a **3-D** dataset — the stride is applied as `[::s, ::s, ::s]` and the budget divides by `s ** 3`, so anything else raises `ValueError` rather than reaching the caller's slicing as an opaque "too many indices" `IndexError`. |
| `decimation_note(step, full_shape)` | The single user-facing sentence for a decimated display load, printing `full_shape` in the dataset's own `(z, y, x)` order so it agrees with the viewer status line's `shape (z, y, x)` rather than contradicting it. |
| `iter_blocks(dset, *, budget_bytes, axis=0)` | Yields `(slice, array)` blocks along `axis`, each within the budget, covering the dataset exactly once in ascending order; a budget smaller than one layer still yields single layers rather than stalling. `axis` accepts **0 or 1** — `axis=1` is what an in-plane (per-column) operation such as a Y-wise median needs; anything else raises `ValueError`. |
| `iter_with_context(blocks, *, trailing=1, axis=0)` | Re-yields an ascending `(slice, array)` stream as `(interior, window, within)`, where `window` is the block with the *next* block's first `trailing` rows appended, `interior` is the block's own range in source coordinates and `within` indexes that range inside `window` — so a consumer writes `window[within]` and never redoes the arithmetic. This is what lets an operation with a **forward-looking local dependency** stream: linear interpolation along Z and `map_coordinates(order=1)` both read the row *after* the one they land on. It takes a *stream of blocks* rather than a dataset deliberately — the blocks needing context are often **generated** (an aligned volume that is never materialised) and so cannot be re-indexed to widen a read; it never re-reads, holding only one block plus the next block's head, which is why it behaves identically over an HDF5 dataset and over a forward-only generator. It looks only forward, by design: a symmetric halo was considered and rejected because nothing here needs one. `axis` must match the axis the stream was blocked along, since "the next rows" means the next rows *along that axis*; a mismatch does **not** raise, because over an `axis=1` stream an axis-0 implementation still recovers every interior correctly (`within` spans the whole of axis 0) and corrupts only the appended context — supplying the successor's first Z-layer where its first column was meant. `within` is a plain slice for `axis=0` and a tuple for `axis=1`; `window[within]` is the interior either way. Raises `ValueError` if a successor cannot supply `trailing` rows — a window one row short corrupts exactly one block edge and hides from the budget-independence tests, since every budget corrupts it identically. That raise is unreachable at the default `trailing=1` (a block always has one row) and **budget-dependent** above it: the final block is a remainder of the block width, so a workstation budget can succeed where a laptop's raises. A caller passing `trailing > 1` must size blocks to exceed the context width or handle the raise. The final block gets no context, which is correct — it ends where the source ends, so its edge behaviour must match the source's. Memory cost is `trailing` rows *above* the block itself, so a budget is exceeded by that much; negligible at the default of one row against any realistic block, but stated, because an unstated approximation in a memory-budget module is how budgets stop being trusted. |
| `BlockReader(dset, budget_bytes, axis=0)` | A dataset too large for the budget, presented as an iterable of `(slice, array)` blocks; carries `.shape`/`.dtype`/`.nbytes` but is deliberately not an ndarray look-alike. |
| `load_or_stream(dset, *, budget_bytes)` | The whole array (`dset[:]`) when it fits the budget, else a `BlockReader`. |
| `NEUMAIER_LANES` | How many independent compensated accumulators `neumaier_sum` keeps (4096). The value at global position *i* of the stream always lands in lane `i % NEUMAIER_LANES`. |
| `NeumaierState` | The continuable state of a compensated sum — one `(total, compensation)` accumulator **per lane**, plus the number of values consumed (`.count`). `.value` is the sum; indexing/unpacking still yields the reduced `(total, compensation)` pair, whose sum is `.value`. Two states compare equal only when their lanes match **bit for bit** and their counts agree — stricter than comparing the sums, which is the point: it catches a lane scheme that regrouped the data and got away with it in the total. Not a plain pair, because a pair cannot be continued without collapsing the lanes, and collapsing at a block boundary would put that boundary back into the answer. |
| `neumaier_sum(values, *, state=None)` | Compensated sum of `values`, returning a `NeumaierState`; pass a previous return value back as `state` to continue across blocks (the state is never mutated). Vectorised: it folds the stream into `NEUMAIER_LANES` lanes with numpy ops rather than looping per element, sending the value at **global** position *i* to lane `i % NEUMAIER_LANES` whatever block it arrived in — an incoming run is split into the values completing the part-finished lane row, the whole rows after it, and the ragged remainder, each folded into the lane range its global indices name. "Global position" means the position in the **flattened stream this function is fed**, which is what makes the lane assignment structural for `axis=0` blocking and merely empirical for `axis=1`: an axis-0 block is a contiguous run of the volume's ravel order, so widening it moves no value's index, while an axis-1 block ravels each row segment separately and indices *do* move with the block width. Measured 15 M float64: **0.097 s vs 2.19 s** for the per-element loop it replaced (~23×) and 0.115 s for `np.nansum`, i.e. the compensated sum is no longer the expensive option. Accuracy is the same class as the order it replaced, and on realistic data the same *bits*: bit-identical to the old per-element order (and to `math.fsum`) on Gaussian, wide-exponent and heavy-tailed samples up to 13.5 M values, on hundreds of random fuzz cases and on every DFXM-shaped volume tried. The two orders are **not** identical in general — constructed catastrophic cancellation separates them, and there neither is correctly rounded, since Neumaier's single compensation term is not exact in that regime — and an input whose partial sums overflow float64 can diverge outright (`[1e308, 1, -1e308, 1] × 5000`: the per-element order cancels pairwise and returns `10000.0`, the lane order overflows one lane and returns `nan`). Both are functions of the data, not of the budget, so budget-independence is untouched; no DFXM quantity comes near either regime. |
| `dataset_blocks(dset, *, budget_bytes, axis=0)` | Just the arrays from `iter_blocks`, without the slices — the adapter that feeds a dataset to the `stream_*` reductions. |
| `stream_mean(blocks)` | Mean of the *finite* values across an iterable of arrays, bit-identical for any blocking (carries the `NeumaierState` across blocks and divides by its `.count`; never `np.mean` per block); `nan` when nothing is finite. Takes an iterable of arrays rather than a dataset because the statistics it serves are over **generated** blocks — the aligned volume a stage never materialises — not over anything on disk. |
| `stream_minmax(blocks)` | `(min, max)` of the *finite* values across an iterable of arrays; `(nan, nan)` when nothing is finite. Free to use `np.min`/`np.max` per block: unlike summation, min and max are partition-invariant in the strict sense the module requires — the answer genuinely does not depend on the grouping. With one exception, now handled: the **sign of a zero**. `-0.0 == 0.0`, so a block-wise `min`/`max` keeps whichever it met first, which is the *blocking* — on `[-0.0]*8 + [0.0]*8 + [1, 2]` this returned `+0.0` over the whole array and `-0.0` at every smaller budget. The rule is now decided by the data: the minimum prefers `-0.0` and the maximum `+0.0` whenever that sign is present among the zeros. The scan runs only for a block whose own min or max is exactly zero, and it is `np.signbit(finite)` — one byte per value — never `np.signbit(finite[finite == 0.0])`, which *copies* the zeros and so costs a second full-size array on precisely the zero-heavy volumes this module exists for (measured 16.8 MB extra on a 16.8 MB block; a memory-budget helper must not exceed its own budget, and `test_stream_minmax_zero_sign_costs_no_extra_copy` asserts the peak rather than trusting the argument). No numpy parity is at stake — `np.min` over mixed zeros is equally arbitrary — and no numerical information either, since the two compare equal; the guarantee is. This is also the one violation of it that `tests/equivalence.py` structurally cannot catch, since `array_equal` calls the two zeros equal — see its own documented blind spot — so `float.hex()` is what the test asserts on. |
| `stream_quantile(make_blocks, q)` | The `q`-th percentile of the *finite* values, **exactly** — the number `np.percentile(finite, q)` returns, not an estimate — in bounded memory; `nan` when nothing is finite. Exactness is the requirement, not a nicety: colour limits are computed this way, so an approximate streaming quantile (cheaper, and equally budget-independent) would shift the colour scaling of every figure re-run against old data, which is why one was rejected in design. Takes a **factory** — a zero-argument callable returning a fresh iterable of arrays — because it traverses more than once, and it traverses *generated* blocks (an aligned volume never materialised) as happily as an HDF5 dataset. This is the one signature difference from `stream_mean`/`stream_minmax`, which take the iterable itself, so `lambda: some_generator` (a factory that returns the *same*, now-exhausted, generator on pass 2) is the easy mistake; it is caught after the counting pass with a `ValueError` naming it, rather than surfacing as a bare "need at least one array to concatenate" out of `_select_rank` several traversals later. Three passes: finite min/max/count; then `_select_rank` histograms the window into `_QUANTILE_BINS` (65536) bins, locates the bin holding the target rank and narrows into it while it holds more than `_QUANTILE_EXACT_CAP` (2²⁰) values; then the survivors of that bin — a small array — are sorted and indexed exactly. Several details carry the exactness. `_lerp` reproduces numpy's two-form interpolation (it switches to `hi - (hi - lo) * (1 - t)` at `t >= 0.5`) **and its dtype**: numpy computes `hi - lo` in the array's own type, so on the float32 volumes this project stores (`paraview.py`'s `SAVE_DTYPE`, `rocking.py`, `slices.py`) a float64 subtraction diverges by ~1e-8 relative wherever the bracketing order statistics differ by more than about 2×. The target rank uses numpy's own `linear`-method virtual index, `(n - 1) * (q / 100)` — the algebraically equal generic Hyndman-Fan form `n*qq + (1 - qq) - 1` differs in the last bits. `_finite64(block)` widens each block's finite values to float64 before any comparison, because numpy 1.x demotes a Python float compared against a float32 array (value-based casting): the float64 bin edge would round, and the survivor mask would stop agreeing with the histogram that counted the value — an empty survivor set at best, a selection from the wrong candidates at worst. The histogram uses `side="right"` with an inclusive final bin, reproducing `np.histogram`'s convention so a value on a bin edge is counted in the same bin the survivor pass collects it from (get this backwards and the maximum falls outside every bin, or the counted bin disagrees with the collected one — an `IndexError` or a silently wrong value). `_bin_edges(lo, hi)` blends (`lo * (1 - t) + hi * t`) rather than calling `np.linspace`, which computes `hi - lo` first and overflows to nan edges on a range wider than float64 can subtract, and clamps the result non-decreasing because `searchsorted` mis-answers unsorted edges silently. `_select_among_ties` is what guarantees **termination**: once the window is about one ulp wide every interior edge rounds onto an endpoint and the chosen bin *is* the whole window, so bisection would repeat forever — a run of more than 2²⁰ identical values (a constant background region in a full-size volume) reaches this — and such a window holds at most a couple of distinct floats, so it is resolved by counting per distinct value. Narrowing goes to the chosen bin's **observed `[min, max]`** (`_observed_bounds`), not to its edges: edges narrow the range geometrically but the *exponent* only linearly, so converging onto a tie at exactly `0.0` walked the whole denormal range — 142 full traversals of a float32 intensity volume against 16 for a background of `1.0`, i.e. hours of reading on the 17 GB paraview volume whose masked voxels are exactly zero, which a user cannot tell from a hang. Observed bounds collapse a tied bin in one round (measured 7 / 6 / 10 traversals for backgrounds of 0.0 / 1.0 / 100.0) for one extra traversal per narrowing, and narrowing is itself rare. `_zero_sign` settles the sign of a returned zero from the data — `-0.0` when every zero present is negative (numpy's answer too), `+0.0` when both signs appear (where numpy's own sign comes from an arbitrary partition order) — because `-0.0 == 0.0` otherwise lets each `min`/`sort`/dict key keep whichever the *blocking* showed it first, a budget-dependent bit that `assert_budget_independent` cannot see and `float.hex()` can. One deliberate divergence from numpy: when the rank needs no interpolation the order statistic is returned directly, so a spread wide enough to overflow `hi - lo` yields the value rather than the `nan` numpy's unconditional lerp produces there. A `q` outside `[0, 100]` raises `ValueError` as numpy does, rather than answering with a clamped (and plausible-looking) min or max. Working set is a few tens of MB above the caller's block whatever the volume's size — the edges and counts, plus one bin's survivors held twice while they are concatenated and sorted, plus (for float32 input) the float64 widening of a block's finite values. |
| `centring_scaffold_bytes(method, n_values)` | What `stream_mean` / `stream_quantile` cost **besides** the caller's block, as `(during_pass, after_pass)` bytes. Blocking-independent, so no block size pays them off: a caller sizing blocks against a budget must add the first to every candidate and treat the second as a floor no budget below it can meet — which is what `alignment.align_volume_streamed` does, and why this lives next to the code that allocates it rather than as a number copied into the caller. `mean`: sixteen `NEUMAIER_LANES` float64 widths for the state, its copy and a fold's lane-sized temporaries (0.52 MB charged, 0.40 MB measured). `median`: four `_QUANTILE_BINS + 1` float64 widths for the edges, the temporaries `_bin_edges` builds them from, the counts and their cumsum (2.10 MB charged, 1.58 MB measured), **plus** one bin's survivors — at most `_QUANTILE_EXACT_CAP` float64, accumulated once during the traversal and then held three times over (list, concatenation, sort) after it. *n_values* caps the survivor term for data too small to reach the cap. The survivor bound is a genuine worst case, approached only when one bin of 65536 holds a million values; ordinary data collects a handful, so a median on a small volume can be told its budget is short when it would in fact have fitted. Sizing against the worst case is deliberate — a budget the data can exceed is not a budget — and the cost lands where it is cheapest (small volumes run one layer at a time in seconds). |
| `block_reduce(dset, fn, *, budget_bytes, init)` | Folds `dset` block-by-block through `fn(acc, block) -> acc`, in ascending block order. |
| `block_nansum(dset, *, budget_bytes)` | Sum of the *finite* values, built on `block_reduce` + `neumaier_sum` (the fold carries a `NeumaierState`, and the result is its `.value`); bit-identical for any `budget_bytes`. Ignores all non-finite values — NaN **and** ±inf (unlike `np.nansum`, which propagates inf; inf would poison the compensation term). On all-finite data it differs from `np.nansum` by up to ~1 ulp — numpy's pairwise reduction order is itself budget-dependent and cannot give the cross-budget guarantee. A dataset with a zero-length axis yields no blocks and sums to `0.0` rather than raising. |
| `two_pass(dset, stat_fn, apply_fn, *, budget_bytes, init)` | Pass 1 folds every block into a global statistic via `stat_fn`; pass 2 yields `(slice, apply_fn(stat, block))` per block — the pattern for e.g. mean-subtraction in bounded memory, at the cost of reading the dataset twice. |
| `scratch_array(shape, dtype, *, dirpath, prefix="dfxm_scratch")` | Context manager yielding a disk-backed `np.memmap` for irreducibly whole-array work (an exact median, a global fit) that cannot be expressed as a block fold — this is what lets that work run on a machine that cannot hold the volume, slower but without refusing. Cleans up on exit *or* on the caller's exception: `flush()` → close `._mmap` (**Windows only** — it refuses to unlink a file still mapped; POSIX unlinks mapped files fine, and skipping the close there means a stale caller reference reads harmless pages instead of segfaulting) → `del` → `gc.collect()` → `os.unlink`; the unlink is wrapped so a cleanup failure never masks the caller's original exception. Two caveats: the array is **dead after the `with` block** — never return or stash it (on Windows a post-exit access is a hard fault); and temporaries still allocate in RAM — `out = a * b + c` on a memmapped `a` materialises a full-size temporary and defeats the purpose, so bucket-3 callers must use in-place ops (`np.multiply(a, b, out=a)`). |

#### `alignment.py`
The **single source of truth** for putting volumes into the shared world frame.
The fixed order is `abs(FWHM) → ROI → samy X-shift → uniform-Z interp → centre`.

| Function / type | What it does |
|---|---|
| `_samy_ref(samy, ref)` | Resolve the X reference: explicit value, else `samy[0]`. |
| `compute_pad_left/right(samy, scale_x, dir, ref_samy=None)` | The left/right canvas padding the X-shift will add (same formula, so an origin can be anchored). |
| `apply_samy_shifts_to_volume(vol, samy, scale_x, dir, ref_samy=None, *, pad=None)` | Sub-pixel shift each Z-layer along image-X by its samy offset, expanding the canvas (NaN-padded). `ref_samy` anchors to an external frame (the rocking stage anchors to mosa). `pad=(left, right)` overrides the canvas growth, which is otherwise derived from this call's `samy`: the pad is a property of the **whole** volume's samy range, so a block-wise caller handing over a slice would get a different width for every block. `None` (every in-core caller) computes it from `samy` exactly as before. |
| `interpolate_to_uniform_z(vol, samz, ref_samz=None, *, z_uniform=None)` | Resample irregular `samz` layers onto a uniform Z grid → `(vol, z_uniform_um, scale_z)`. `z_uniform` supplies the target grid instead of deriving it from this call's `samz` — the Z-grid twin of `pad`, since a block's own samz implies a different grid. `None` derives it exactly as before. |
| `_z_positions_um` / `_median_z_step` / `_z_grid(samz, n_layers, ref_samz)` | The grid arithmetic, factored out so `interpolate_to_uniform_z` and the streamed path cannot drift apart. `_z_grid` returns `(z_um, z_uniform_um, scale_z)` from the **motor arrays alone** — no voxel is read, which is what lets a streaming caller size its output up front. An **empty `samz`** now yields empty arrays rather than raising `IndexError` out of `z_um.min()`, as the old inline code did. |
| `apply_roi_3d(data, roi_x, roi_y)` | Crop a `(Z,Y,X)` volume in pixel coords. |
| `_center_offset(blocks_factory, method)` | The centring statistic, as one shared definition: `volumeio.stream_mean` for `mean`, `volumeio.stream_quantile(…, 50.0)` for `median`. Both the in-core and streamed paths come through here, which is what makes them bit-identical rather than nearly so. Costs the ulp-scale drift from `np.nanmean` accepted in the design — a handful of ulps, on the more accurate side, and larger in ulp terms the closer the mean sits to zero (measured 41 ulps from `np.nanmean` on a 13.5 M-voxel centred volume, where the compensated value is the exactly-rounded one and numpy's pairwise order is not); `median` is bit-equal to `np.nanmedian`. The **time** cost is closed: `neumaier_sum` is vectorised over `NEUMAIER_LANES` accumulators, so the sum of 13.5 M finite voxels costs ~0.09 s rather than ~2.2 s, and `align_volume(center_method="mean")` on a 60×300×400 volume went 1.28 s → 0.84 s (the residual is the second alignment pass the offset forces, ~0.38 s, not the summation). The vectorisation moved no measured value: the centring offset is bit-identical before and after. |
| `center_around_zero(data, method)` | Subtract the `mean`/`median` over finite voxels → `(data, offset)`, via `_center_offset`. An all-non-finite volume is returned untouched with offset `0.0` — but the method name is now **validated first**, so a typo raises `ValueError` on such a volume instead of being silently accepted (it previously returned early). This matches the streamed path, which validates before reading anything. |
| `raw_detector_origin(samy, z, …)` | World origin (µm) placing a volume in the raw-detector-absolute frame (folds in samy pad + ROI + darfix origin). |
| `AlignedVolume` + `align_volume(...)` | The whole fixed pipeline in one call. Now the **in-core façade** over `align_volume_streamed`: it runs the stream with `budget_bytes=None` — "one block, whatever it costs" — and adopts that block as its result (no second full-size copy). One implementation, not two to keep in step. Signature, return type and results are unchanged. (It used to ask for `volume.nbytes * 8`, a guess at a budget big enough for one block; once `budget_bytes` came to mean the whole working set that guess no longer covered it, and a caller that materialises the entire volume anyway has no business pretending to a budget.) |
| `StreamedAlignment` | The aligned volume as a **re-traversable stream of Z-blocks**: `shape`, `dtype`, `z_uniform_um`, `scale_z_um`, `pad_left`, `pad_right`, `center_offset`, `block_layers`, `working_set_bytes` — all known before a voxel is read, so a writer can size its output up front — plus `blocks`, a **factory** returning a fresh `Iterator[(slice, ndarray)]` in ascending output-Z order. Traversing twice re-runs the alignment chain. `block_layers` is how many output layers a block carries and `working_set_bytes` is what the model says producing one peaks at — the number `budget_bytes` was checked against, so a caller can see what its budget actually bought. |
| `align_volume_streamed(dset, samy, samz, *, scale_x, samy_direction=1, roi_x=None, roi_y=None, take_abs=False, center_method=None, budget_bytes, scratch_dir=None)` | The same fixed chain, block by block, **calling the same step functions** — so there is one implementation of the arithmetic. `dset` is anything that slices and has `shape`/`dtype` (h5py dataset, memmap, array). The two quantities a step would otherwise re-derive per block (the samy canvas pad, the Z grid) are computed once from the motor arrays and passed in via `pad=`/`z_uniform=`; that is what keeps every block on one canvas. Bit-identical for any `budget_bytes`. An **empty `samy`** skips the X-shift instead of raising `IndexError` out of `_samy_ref`, which is what the old in-core code did. An empty **`samz`** is *not* handled — the Z grid comes from the motor positions, so a caller with none must supply its own answer (see `paraview.py`'s `_unaligned_field`). The block generator `del`s its input span and its pre-centring block before yielding: a generator frame stays alive between `yield`s, so anything still bound is retained for as long as the consumer holds the block — invisible with one stream, and four whole raw volumes on top of four blocks when a caller keeps one stream open per field, as `paraview` does (measured: 144 MB of a 476 MB peak on a four-field 128×192×192 export). |
| `budget_bytes` — **what it bounds** | The **working set**: everything the chain holds at once to produce a block, not the block's own bytes. Until the `_WorkingSet` model replaced it, blocks were sized by `budget_bytes // (ny * nx * itemsize)`, i.e. so the *output block* fit — and the measured peak came out **3.6×–7.7× the budget** across ordinary shapes, ROIs, dtypes and centring modes, *worse the tighter the budget* (the input span and the fixed temporaries do not shrink with the block, and the blocking floors at one layer). With the model, a ~90-case sweep — shapes, f32/f64, ROI, `take_abs`, wide and narrow pads, dense-Z, plateau, bimodal-Z, ascending/descending/shuffled samz, in-memory/h5py/memmap, all three centring modes, budgets /1 to /64 — measures **0.23–0.77×** in every case that is not floored, and no non-floored case above 1.0. The upper figure is the load-bearing one — it is what says the budget is a budget — and it is what a caller should size against; the wide low side is expected, not slack to reclaim, because `centring_scaffold_bytes`'s survivor term is a worst case (one bin of 65536 holding a million values) that ordinary data comes nowhere near, so `median` on untied data lands at the bottom of the range, 0.23–0.35. `None` means "one block, whatever it costs" (the in-core façade). Floor: one output layer plus the blocking-independent scaffolding — if even that overruns the budget the run proceeds, `warnings.warn`s, and reports the overrun in `working_set_bytes`, because on this pipeline a slow stage beats a stage that refuses to start. **These are `tracemalloc` figures — Python-level allocations, not RSS.** h5py's chunk cache, a memmap's resident pages and allocator fragmentation are outside every number here; the project separately measured modelled peak running ~1.66× under real peak RSS on the real dataset, and that gap is *not* what this model closes. |
| `_WorkingSet` | The model, counted from the code rather than fitted: per **input** layer, the span read (`0` when `dset` is a plain `ndarray`, whose slice is a view; charged for h5py and memmap, and again for `np.abs` when `take_abs`) plus the padded samy canvas **× 3** — the canvas, `interp1d(copy=True)`'s copy of it, and the permuted copy `np.take(y, ind, axis)` builds while that one is alive, since scipy 1.11.4's `interp1d.__init__` sorts **unconditionally** (`assume_sorted` is never passed); per **output** layer, `ny*nx*(2*in_itemsize + 3*out_itemsize)` for `scipy._call_linear`'s five block-sized arrays, one block the consumer's loop variable is still holding across the `next()` that computes the following one, and — when centring — what the statistic leaves bound across that same `next()` (`finite` for the mean; `finite`, `window` and the searchsorted indices for the median). `fixed` adds the samy loop's per-layer temporaries and `volumeio.centring_scaffold_bytes`'s "during pass" figure; `floor` is that function's "after pass" figure, taken as a **maximum** against everything else rather than added, since the quantile's survivors peak once the traversal is over and the chain's temporaries are gone. Still **excluded**: whatever the *consumer* accumulates (a caller writing every block into one array is allocating the whole output by choice), and anything that never passes through the Python allocator. |
| `_sorted_z(z_um)` / `_max_input_span(z_um, z_uniform, step, sorted_z)` / `_solve_out_step(nz, z_um, z_uniform, cost, budget_bytes, sorted_z)` | Solving the blocking. `_max_input_span` is the **exact** widest span any block of a given step reads (not a bound on it), which is what keeps the budget honest when the span is nothing like the block. `_solve_out_step` bisects on the block size because the working set is a step function of it through that span; every step it accepts has been priced, so a non-monotonic span can only make the answer conservative, never wrong. `_sorted_z` hoists `_input_span`'s mergesort so one sort serves the whole call — every candidate the search prices *and* every block the stream then yields. |
| `_input_span(z_um, z_target, *, sorted_z=None)` | Which **input** layers a block's output layers need, as a half-open range in original (on-disk) layer order. Mirrors `interp1d`'s own bracket, `(idx-1, idx)` with `idx = clip(searchsorted(sorted_z, target, "left"), 1, n-1)` — note the `-1`: a target sitting exactly **on** a sample node is still interpolated from the pair below it, so spanning only the node hands `interp1d` a single point. Copes with decreasing or non-monotonic `samz` by working in the sorted order (mergesort, matching `interp1d`'s own stable sort) and then spanning `[min, max]` of the original indices it selected — never a wrong answer, since adding input layers cannot loosen a bracket. On a **non-monotonic** samz that span can degenerate to the whole array, so one output block reads the entire input volume. That is **no longer a budget hole**: `_solve_out_step` asks this function for the span of every candidate blocking before reading anything, so the degenerate span is priced in and simply buys fewer output layers per block (in the limit, the one-layer floor and a warning). A merely *decreasing* samz is fine (reversing keeps neighbours adjacent) and raster samz is monotone by construction. |

**Centring costs an extra traversal** in the streamed path, because the statistic
is over the *aligned* volume (NaN-padded canvas, interpolated Z grid) and cannot
be precomputed from the source. `mean` is one extra pass. `median` traverses
several times, so the aligned volume is cached first — in RAM when the solved
blocking is a single block (`out_step == nz`, which is what the in-core façade's
`budget_bytes=None` always gives), else to
`volumeio.scratch_array(dirpath=scratch_dir)`; with no `scratch_dir` and no room
it falls back to re-running the chain per pass — slower, never failed.

Reading a scratch cache **back** is inside `budget_bytes` too, and is priced
rather than assumed cheap. A memmap slice costs nothing itself, so this branch
originally handed `budget_bytes` straight to `dataset_blocks` — but the consumer
is `stream_quantile`, which holds per element of the block what `_WorkingSet`
charges as `stat_layer` (`finite`, `window`, the `searchsorted(...) - 1` indices
and `np.clip`'s separate output, plus the `isfinite` mask), and the blocking-
independent scaffolding on top. Measured on a 40×200×260 float32 median, peak as
a multiple of the budget: 0.85 / **1.68** / **3.36** / **3.70** / **5.20** at
`ws`÷2, ÷4, ÷8, ÷16, ÷128 — rising as the budget shrank, the same signature the
working-set model exists to remove, on the branch a 17 GB volume actually takes.
The cache read is now sized as `(budget − scaffold) × itemsize ÷ (itemsize +
8·(retained+1) + 1)`, i.e. the block bytes that leave room for the consumer, one
lane above `stat_layer` because this pass has no generator to hand back to and so
holds `clip`'s input and output together. After: **0.70 / 0.80 / 0.71 / 0.42 /
1.67**, the last two floored cases (0.31 and 0.15 of their own reported working
set). `test_streamed_peak_stays_within_the_budget` carries `scratch_dir` rows so
the branch stays inside the measured matrix.

#### `raster.py`
Per-layer sample positions.
- `find_h5_file(folder)` — the `.h5` in a folder (`folder.h5`, else smallest `*.h5`).
- `extract_motor_positions(folders, samy_path, samz_path)` — scalar `(samy, samz)` per folder → `(samy_arr, samz_arr, names)`; folders without motors are skipped.
- `nearest_index(samy_arr, samz_arr, ty, tz)` — index of the layer closest to a target `(samy, samz)` (used by [[#matched.py]]).

#### `cmaps.py`
ParaView colormaps not shipped with matplotlib. Holds the authoritative *Fast*
control points (9 sRGB points, `ColorSpace: "Lab"`, from ParaView master
`Remoting/Views/ColorMaps.json`), converts them to CIELAB, interpolates
linearly on the normalized positions (exactly how ParaView renders the map) and
bakes a 256-entry `ListedColormap` named `"fast"`. `fast_colormap(n=256)`
builds it; `register()` registers it with `matplotlib.colormaps` (idempotent)
and is called once at [[#plotting.py]] import, so `"fast"` resolves everywhere
— matplotlib, the export dialogs, and pyvista alike.

#### `plotting.py`
GUI-safe plotting helpers — **never** `pyplot`/`matplotlib.use`.

- `symmetric_limits(data, percentile=None)` — colour limits symmetric about 0.
- `round_limits_outward(vmin, vmax)` — round colour limits outward (vmin down, vmax up) to "nice" 2-significant-digit values (step = 0.5 × 10^floor(log10|v|)), so evenly spaced colourbar ticks land on round numbers. Symmetric input stays exactly symmetric. Zero, non-finite, or degenerate inputs are returned unchanged.
- `apply_round_clim(vmin, vmax, style)` → `(vmin, vmax, note | None)` — call `round_limits_outward` when `style.round_clim` is set. Returns a human-readable note string describing what changed (e.g. `"colour limits rounded ±0.0778 → ±0.08 (round_clim)"`) or `None` when rounding is off/not needed. Stages surface the note in the run log and results summary.
- `physical_extent(shape, px, py, roi)` — imshow `extent` in µm.
- `get_cmap(name)` — colormap lookup; ParaView's `"fast"` is a real registered colormap (see [[#cmaps.py]]), so no fallback is needed.
- `new_figure(figsize)` — a white `Figure` (no pyplot).
- `styled_figure(figsize, *, styled)` — the Figure constructor every shared builder uses. `styled=True` (a `PlotStyle` is in play) turns on matplotlib constrained layout, which measures every text element at its final font size and reserves space so title/axis-labels/colourbar/offset-text can never overlap — the figure keeps its exact requested size and the axes shrink instead. `styled=False` is the legacy path: a plain `Figure`, byte-identical with the pre-export renderers. `build_companion_figure` (profiles) is the one exception — it always passes `styled=True` on both its dispatch paths; the fixed-scale path (`_build_companion_fixed`) immediately calls `fig.set_layout_engine("none")` afterwards, since `plotting.place_axes_stack` does its own deterministic sizing and constrained layout would fight it.

**Publication-export primitives** (new; all accept a `PlotStyle` argument):

| Symbol | What it does |
|---|---|
| `PlotStyle` | Dataclass holding every style knob — scale bar (show / length / thickness / label-scale / location / edge-inset `scale_bar_inset_pt` in printed points / colour / box show+colour+alpha+margin), text (font_scale / `title_scale` / show_title / center_axis_labels / `axes_mode` — `"full"`/`"no_frame"`/`"none"`, default `"full"`, axes decoration on map figures via `apply_axes_mode` below), colourbar (show / label / fraction / ticks / `round_clim`; typography 2026-07-25: `cbar_label_scale` and `cbar_tick_scale` multiply `font_scale` independently on the label's 10 pt and the tick numbers' 9 pt bases, `cbar_labelpad_pt` sets the label↔tick-numbers gap in printed points with `None` = matplotlib default), figure (figure_width, `scale_um_per_cm`), output (formats / dpi) and **per-quantity colormaps** (`cmap_mosa_com="fast"`, `cmap_mosa_fwhm="magma"`, `cmap_strain="RdBu_r"`, `cmap_raw="gray"`, looked up via `cmap_for(group)`; groups in `CMAP_GROUPS`, curated dropdown list in `CMAP_CHOICES`). `None` in any builder means "use legacy look". `title_scale` multiplies the title font size independently of `font_scale`. `round_clim=True` routes auto-computed colour limits through `apply_round_clim` in the slices, strain, visualize, rocking, and matched stages (matched rounds only when both `vmin`/`vmax` params are blank). Per-group colourbar number format is controlled by `tickfmt_<group>` / `offset_scale_<group>` / `offset_pos_<group>` fields (one per `CMAP_GROUPS` entry), looked up via `tickfmt_for(group)` / `offset_scale_for(group)` / `offset_pos_for(group)`; `group=None` returns the neutral defaults (`"auto"` / `1.0` / `"top"`). `scale_um_per_cm: float \| None = None` is an opt-in fixed physical scale (µm of data per cm of page) — when set it overrides `figure_width` for maps, and the profiles trace figures size their box from the line length at the TRACE-effective scale (`trace_width_in` ignored; the companion stays excluded). `trace_scale_um_per_cm: float \| None = None` is the trace-only override: blank = traces follow `scale_um_per_cm`, read via `trace_fixed_scale(style)` (trace value when positive-finite, else the `fixed_scale` fallback — traces typically want ~half the map value or less). Read defensively via `fixed_scale`/`trace_fixed_scale`/`fixed_scale_box` below, never the fields directly. |
| `PUBLICATION_STYLE` | A ready-made `PlotStyle` tuned for publication: white scale bar with a box, font_scale=2.2, colourbar_ticks=5, single-column width, PNG+PDF+SVG at 300 dpi, and per-group tick formats (strain → scientific with the ×10ⁿ exponent at the **bottom**, mosaicity → auto, raw → arbitrary units). |
| `figure_size(style, ext_x, ext_y)` | Returns `(w, h)` in inches from `style.figure_width` (`"single"`=3.5 in, `"double"`=7.0 in), preserving the physical aspect ratio plus ~1 in headroom; returns `None` for `"auto"`. |
| `fixed_scale(style)` | Defensive read of `style.scale_um_per_cm`: `None` for `style=None`, a missing attribute, a blank/non-numeric/non-positive/non-finite value (stale persisted strings like `"50"` still parse; `"junk"` degrades to `None` like any other malformed style field), else the positive float. Never raises. |
| `fixed_scale_box(style, ext_x_um, ext_y_um, scale=None)` | Target axes-box `(w_in, h_in, effective_um_per_cm)` for fixed-scale mode: `w_in, h_in = ext_x_um/scale/2.54, ext_y_um/scale/2.54`. `scale` (an already-validated µm/cm, e.g. from `trace_fixed_scale`) overrides the style's own `scale_um_per_cm`; `None` keeps the `fixed_scale(style)` read. Returns `None` when the effective scale is `None` or either extent is non-positive/non-finite (degenerate — caller keeps its own sizing). `trace_fixed_scale(style)` (defined beside `fixed_scale`) is the TRACE-effective reader: `trace_scale_um_per_cm` when positive-finite, else the `fixed_scale` fallback; never raises. A typo scale that would request a side over `_MAX_FIXED_SIDE_IN` (30 in) is clamped to 30 in preserving aspect, with the effective µm/cm raised accordingly and a `logging.warning` — never an exception or an oversized render. |
| `fit_axes_to_box(fig, ax, w_in, h_in, tol_in=0.02, max_iter=3)` | Draw–measure–resize helper: attaches a `FigureCanvasAgg` if the canvas can't render, then draws, measures `ax.get_window_extent()`, and corrects the figure size ADDITIVELY by the miss between the measured axes box and `(w_in, h_in)` — decorations (title/colourbar/labels) are constant in inches, so the first correction is nearly exact and the loop (`max_iter`, default 3) is insurance. The target box must carry the data aspect so `aspect="equal"` doesn't fight the fit. Returns `True` once the miss is within `tol_in` on both axes; on non-convergence keeps the last (finite, floor-clamped ≥0.5 in) size, logs at `INFO`, and returns `False` — never raises. |
| `finalize_fixed_scale(fig, ax, style, ext_x_um, ext_y_um)` | Convenience wrapper: computes `fixed_scale_box(style, ext_x_um, ext_y_um)` and, when set, calls `fit_axes_to_box` with it; a no-op (returns `None`) when the fixed-scale knob is off or the box is degenerate. Currently has no in-tree callers — the stage builders call `fixed_scale_box` + `fit_axes_to_box` directly (they need `box[2]`, the effective µm/cm, for the scale bar); this wrapper is kept as the documented convenience for future stage authors and is covered on-path by `test_finalize_fixed_scale_fits_axes_box_on_path`. |

**Deterministic axes placement** (the trace/companion fixed-scale engine — no iteration, no `set_box_aspect`; replaces `fit_axes_to_box` where an EXACT box is required, e.g. small boxes at large fonts that defeated the iterative fit):

| Symbol | What it does |
|---|---|
| `AxesMargins(left, right, top, bottom)` | Frozen dataclass: one axes' decoration extents (labels/ticks/title/offset text), in inches. `.max_with(other)` returns the per-side max — the shared-margins case for a figure set that must all line up. |
| `measure_axes_margins(fig, ax, extras=(), pad_in=0.02)` | Draws once, then measures `ax`'s `AxesMargins` from `ax.get_tightbbox()` vs `ax.get_window_extent()`. Since 2026-08-18 it also unions in the TRUE window extents of the x/y axis labels (only when `ax.axison` and the axis + label are visible with non-empty text): `Axes.get_tightbbox` measures axis labels "for layout only" — squashed to 1 px in the label's running direction — so a label wider (x) / taller (y) than its axes silently overflowed into the neighbouring cell (real-data finding: an autoscaled narrow trace's "distance along line (µm)" ran into the next column). The corollary is that a y label taller than a short panel now reserves vertical room — the cure is a smaller trace font scale, not overlap. `extras` are additional axes (e.g. a manually placed colourbar) whose tight bbox is unioned in, so they count toward this axes' decoration envelope. `pad_in` adds a small breathing margin on every side. |
| `apply_axes_margins(fig, ax, w_in, h_in, m)` | Sizes `fig` to exactly `(m.left + w_in + m.right, m.bottom + h_in + m.top)` and pins `ax`'s position there — no iteration. |
| `place_axes_box(fig, ax, w_in, h_in, margins=None, pad_in=0.02)` | Gives `ax` an exactly `(w_in, h_in)`-inch box. `margins=None`: places provisionally at the final box size (so tick density is measured at the real geometry) via `apply_axes_margins` with a rough guess, measures the real margins with `measure_axes_margins`, then applies them for real. Explicit `margins` (e.g. `AxesMargins.max_with` across a figure set) applies directly. Returns the margins used. |
| `trace_height_cm(style)` | Defensive read of `style.trace_height_cm`: positive finite float, else the `3.0` default (same never-raises pattern as `fixed_scale`). |
| `trace_fixed_box(style, length_um)` | Target trace-panel box `(w_in, h_in, effective_um_per_cm)`, or `None` when no TRACE-effective scale is set (`trace_fixed_scale(style)` is `None`) or `length_um` is non-positive/non-finite. `w_in = length_um/scale/2.54`; `h_in = min(trace_height_cm(style)/2.54, 30 in)` — the fixed height, NOT `trace_aspect` (which fixed-scale mode ignores). Width clamps to 30 in like the map figures, raising the effective scale and logging a warning. Height also clamps to 30 in (only reachable via an extreme `trace_height_cm`) — it does not affect the effective scale, but the clamp is logged too, so it is never silent. |
| `measured_box_in(fig, ax)` | The axes box as actually rendered, in inches (draws once, reads `ax.get_window_extent()`). Used to verify a placement landed on target. |
| `box_drift_note(label, fig, ax, w_in, h_in, rel_tol=0.005)` | `None` when the rendered box (`measured_box_in`) is within `rel_tol` of `(w_in, h_in)`; else a user-facing string (`"{label}: plot box rendered {w}x{h} cm, expected {w_in}x{h_in} cm — physical scale is off"`), also logged at `WARNING`. Callers append the note to a `notes` list (surfaced in the GUI Results tab). Never raises. |
| `place_axes_stack(fig, panels, pad_in=0.02, gap_in=0.15)` | Stack `panels` top→bottom, each with an EXACT `(w_in, h_in)` box, sharing one left margin (the max over panels) so their boxes left-align. `panels` is `[(ax, w_in, h_in, extras, sync), ...]`: `extras` are attached axes (e.g. a manual colourbar) counted in that panel's decoration envelope (passed to `measure_axes_margins`); `sync`, when not `None`, is `callable(fig, ax)` invoked after each placement pass to re-glue an attachment (e.g. reposition a manual colourbar beside its map panel). Two passes: (1) provisional placement at final box sizes on a generously oversized canvas (so tick density is measured at real geometry), then measure every panel's margins; (2) final placement — figure sized to `shared_left + max(box_w + right_margin)` wide by `sum(top_margin + box_h + bottom_margin) + gap_in*(n-1)` tall, each panel positioned at its own top margin below the previous panel's bottom margin + `gap_in`. Used by `dfxm.stages.profiles._build_companion_fixed` to stack the fixed-scale companion's map + trace panels. |
| `auto_scale_bar_length_um(ext_x)` | A "nice" bar length ≈15% of the X extent, snapped to the 1–2–5–10 series. |
| `draw_scale_bar(ax, length_um=None, *, style, fixed_scale_um_per_cm=None)` | Draw a µm scale bar on `ax` whose data coordinates are in µm. Built as an `AnchoredOffsetbox` in `ax.artists` (`VPacker`: bold label `TextArea` over a bar `Rectangle` in an `AuxTransformBox(transData)`): the optional background box is laid out at draw time around the *rendered* label + bar, so it hugs its content at any `font_scale` (exact under constrained layout), and label/bar are mutually centred. `length_um=None` calls `auto_scale_bar_length_um`. Box padding (`scale_bar_box_margin_pt`) is in real points and applies only while the box is shown; the corner inset is `scale_bar_inset_pt` in real printed points (`borderpad = max(inset_pt, 0)/label_size`; default 15 pt ≡ the former fixed 1.5 font units at default fonts, but no longer growing with `font_scale`). Robust against hand-written styles: non-canonical `scale_bar_loc` strings fall back via the old substring rule, and the label size is floored so `font_scale=0` can't divide by zero. `set_in_layout(False)` keeps constrained layout from budgeting figure margin for it, and every packed artist is clipped to the axes (`AnchoredOffsetbox` ignores its own clip flag), so extreme font scales truncate at the axes edge like the old clipped label. `fixed_scale_um_per_cm` is a keyword-only, opt-in-per-call override for bar **height**: when a positive value is given, `bh = style.scale_bar_thickness_pt * (2.54/72.0) * fixed_scale_um_per_cm` (true printed points at that known µm-per-cm scale) instead of the default `abs(yr) * 0.004 * style.scale_bar_thickness_pt`. The function never infers the scale from `style` itself — only a caller that has actually fit the axes to a physical page size via `finalize_fixed_scale` may pass it; an un-fitted caller must leave it `None`. Left `None` (the default), geometry is byte-identical to before this knob existed. |
| `apply_text_scale(ax, style)` | Scale axis-label/tick fonts by `style.font_scale` and the title by the independent `style.title_scale`; apply `show_title` and `center_axis_labels`. Also grows the title's `pad` proportionally to `font_scale` — a colourbar's scientific-notation offset text sits just above the axes and constrained layout does not account for it when reserving room for the title, so without extra pad the two collide at large font scales. |
| `apply_axes_mode(ax, style)` | Hide map-axes decoration per `style.axes_mode`: `"no_frame"` hides the four spines (ticks/labels stay), `"none"` calls `ax.set_axis_off()`; `"full"` or any stale/unknown persisted value is a no-op (defensive, like `fixed_scale`). The canonical mode list is the module-level `AXES_MODES` tuple (`"full"`, `"no_frame"`, `"none"`), which the GUI combo derives its entries and stale-value guard from (like `CMAP_CHOICES`). Map axes only — never applied to trace, companion, histogram or diagnostic axes. |
| `SCALE_BAR_LOCS: tuple[str, ...]` | The four scale-bar corner locations, canonical order `("lower right", "lower left", "upper right", "upper left")` — `gui/widgets/export_dialog.py`'s `_LOCS` (the `StyleControls` "Bar location" combo) and `gui/figure_builder.py`'s compose-pane "Corner" combo both derive their entries from this, like `AXES_MODES`/`CMAP_CHOICES` above. |
| `colorbar_tick_values(vmin, vmax, n)` | `n` evenly-spaced tick values from vmin..vmax (always includes both endpoints). |
| `add_colorbar(fig, im, ax, label, style, *, group=None, cax=None)` | Add a colourbar honouring `style.colorbar_fraction`, label, tick count, and the per-group number format `style.tickfmt_for(group)`. `group` is one of `CMAP_GROUPS` (or `None` = neutral). Formats: `"auto"`/digit as before; `"scientific"` draws a custom, styleable `×10ⁿ` exponent label at the group's `offset_pos_*` (top/bottom) and `offset_scale_*` size, hiding matplotlib's built-in top offset; `"arb"` drops all ticks and appends " (arb. units)" to the label (unless it already says a.u./arb, or a manual `colorbar_label` override is set). `cax`, when given, draws into that already-placed axes (`fig.colorbar(im, cax=cax)`) instead of stealing space from `ax` (`fig.colorbar(im, ax=ax, fraction=..., pad=...)`) — used by callers (the profiles fixed-scale companion) that place and re-glue the colourbar axes themselves; omitting `cax` (every map builder) is byte-identical to before. Typography: the label draws at `10 * font_scale * cbar_label_scale` pt with `labelpad=cbar_labelpad_pt` (None = matplotlib default) and the tick numbers at `9 * font_scale * cbar_tick_scale` pt. |
